This is a System F parametricity benchmark. We need to prove that any closed term of type `forall X, X -> X`, applied to any closed value and closed well-formed type, reduces (multi-step) to the value.

With nondeterministic choice, the semantics is a relation; we need to find a reduction path. The standard approach: logical relation for System F (parametricity), using a PER or a "may" relation with nondeterminism.

Actually the theorem is a "free theorem" style result: a closed term of type ∀X. X→X applied to U and v reduces to v. Under nondeterminism, we need existence of a reduction path. Let's think.

Key insight: There is a well-known proof using the "logical relation" with a "step-indexed" or "operational" semantics. Since nondeterminism is present, the relation should be a "may" relation: `t ~^T v` meaning t can reduce to ... hmm.

Actually, let's think about the simplest approach: a syntactic normalization/parametricity proof.

The target is: `tm_app (tm_tapp t U) v -->* v`.

Standard proof for pure System F: define logical relation `R_T` on terms with respect to a type substitution ρ mapping type variables to types, such that `R_{X}` relates a value v to itself if it's closed of type ... Hmm. Actually the standard parametricity result for `forall X. X -> X` being the identity: The logical relation interpretation of `∀X. X→X` on terms: `t ∈ R_{∀X.X→X}` iff for all types U and values v with `v ∈ R_U`, `t U` applied to v reduces to v. But we need to show the relation is inhabited by all well-typed closed terms (fundamental theorem / identity extension).

With nondeterminism, the relation must be defined carefully to be closed under reduction, or we can define the logical relation in terms of multi-step reduction to related values.

Note that the definition of `C_{Ty_All T'}` quantifies over U1,U2,R. R is a relation between values of U1 and U2; semantically arbitrary. But then `C_{T'}(ρ[X↦R])` recursively is defined by the structure of T'. But T' may be `Ty_Arrow (Ty_BVar 0) (Ty_BVar 0)`, which contains `Ty_BVar 0`, and we need to interpret bvar 0 as R. Since we can choose X fresh for the finite set of fvars in T' and free in ρ relevant atoms, fine. But the definition of C for a source type T must be well-defined by recursion on T. In the All case, we recurse on `open_ty T' (FVar X)` which is not a subterm of T, so Coq's guard checker rejects a plain Fixpoint... unless we define C with a fuel parameter (e.g., size of T) or use a well-founded recursion on the size of T. Or define C as an inductive family indexed by T! 

Standard approach in Coq: define the logical relation as an Inductive or Fixpoint on the type with a fuel parameter. Using fuel is easy: `C (n : nat) (T : ty) (ρ) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop`, defined by recursion on n, interpreting the type with a "type size" or just using n as fuel decremented at each type constructor. But the universal case recurses on the opened body; fuel decrements. At the end, we need enough fuel for the actual types involved. Since types are finite, we can use fuel = size(T) or a large bound. But the fundamental theorem's IH would give `C n (open_ty T' (FVar X)) ...` for all n perhaps, and the universal definition uses fuel n at T' — need to ensure enough fuel. We can define the relation with a "level" that ensures monotonicity in fuel, then take the limit. This is doable but adds proof burden.

Alternative: define the semantic relation as an inductive predicate indexed by the type (inductive family). In Coq, inductive families can have recursive occurrences with *any* index expression, so universal case can recurse on `open_ty T' (Ty_FVar X)`! E.g.:

```
Inductive rel : ty -> (atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) -> tm -> tm -> Prop := ...
```

But the arrow case's definition needs a universal quantification over arguments and the *term relation* at T1 (negative position?) — `V_{T1→T2}(f1,f2) := ∀ a1 a2, C_{T1}(ρ) a1 a2 -> C_{T2}(ρ) (f1 a1) (f2 a2)`. If C is an Inductive family, then in the constructor for arrow we have `(forall a1 a2, C T1 ρ a1 a2 -> C T2 ρ (tm_app f1 a1) (tm_app f2 a2))` — recursive occurrence in a negative position (to the left of an arrow within the constructor argument). Coq strictly positive check: `C T1 ρ a1 a2 -> ...` — C appears as an argument of an arrow in a premise of a constructor. Is that strictly positive? The occurrence is to the left of `->`, i.e., in a negative position. Coq rejects non-strictly-positive inductive definitions. So inductive family won't work directly for the arrow case. We can use a "semantic type" record with a carrier Prop that's a Fixpoint via a *size* measure.

Fuel approach seems simplest. Let me think about defining the relation by recursion on a natural number "rank" that's an upper bound on the type's size, with monotonicity so that we can decrease/increase as needed.

Actually, there's a standard trick: define the relation by recursion on the *type* using a well-founded relation based on the size of the type, but the All case opens the body with a fvar; `open_ty T' (FVar X)` has the same size as T'? Let's see: open_ty_rec replaces bvar 0 with FVar X, but under nested binders it leaves indices; the size (number of type constructors) is preserved? `open_ty_rec k U T`: replacing `Ty_BVar i` with U when i=k — U is a Ty_FVar, which has size 1, while Ty_BVar i has size 1. So size is preserved under open_ty when U is a fvar! Great: `ty_size (open_ty T (FVar X)) = ty_size T`. But the body T' has size strictly less than `Ty_All T'`, so recursion on size decreases. But another subtlety: The universal relation definition with the "forall X U1 U2 R" means the relation at a universal type depends on *all* X, including ones not fresh. If ρ already maps X to something, we override with R. The body type `open_ty T' (FVar X)` may contain FVar Y for Y ≠ X; ρ handles them. If X happens to equal some Y occurring in T', we override the binding for X which would be wrong. For the universal semantics, the standard definition quantifies over types U1,U2 and relations R. Here X is just a meta-level name for the bound type variable; using a fresh one each time. Quantifying over all X (including non-fresh) makes the relation stronger, which could break the fundamental theorem... Actually the fundamental theorem at T_TAbs uses the IH with a fresh X, and the goal is the relation at ∀ which quantifies over all X. Alternative: avoid the freshness issue by using a de Bruijn *environment of relations* for bound type variables. That is, define the semantics of types with an environment for bvars: `ξ : nat -> rel` plus `ρ : atom -> rel`? But then opening the body with FVar X and using a semantic environment where FVar X is interpreted as R reproduces exactly the de Bruijn semantics. Let's define the term relation by recursion on the type *with de Bruijn environment*:

`C (T : ty) (δ : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (ρ : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop`

where δ interprets de Bruijn indices (index i ↦ nth i δ), and the All case extends δ with R and recurses on T' (a strict subterm!). This is structurally recursive on T — Coq accepts it! The universal value relation would be:

`V_All: ∃ s1 s2, t1 -->* tm_tabs s1 ∧ t2 -->* tm_tabs s2 ∧ ∀ U1 U2 (R : tm -> tm -> Prop), C T' (R :: δ) ρ (open_tm_ty s1 U1) (open_tm_ty s2 U2)`.

Note: `open_tm_ty s1 U1` substitutes bvar 0 with U1; but the body T' has bvar 0 for the bound type variable, and we interpret bvar 0 as R in the environment. All good — no freshness issues! The type T' contains bvars at various depths; for the recursion into T' we extend δ with R at the front. But wait: the body term s1's type annotations contain bvars too, but we don't interpret term type annotations; the relation only depends on T (the index type) — the term is untyped. When we later have nested type applications inside the term, those are terms; the term relation doesn't inspect type arguments' semantic meaning. So the type index T is the only thing guiding the semantics, and de Bruijn environment δ works.

But careful: when the universal relation instantiates the body at U1, U2, the type of the resulting term is `open_ty T' U1` on the left. But our relation C at T' with δ = R::δ' doesn't involve U1 at all. The term relation is untyped, so it doesn't need U1; the semantics of bvar 0 is R which is the relation we choose. That's exactly the standard parametricity: instantiate X at R, and the terms happen to be typed at U1,U2 but the relation is untyped. Fine.

Let me think about the T_TAbs case more concretely with the de Bruijn environment approach and see if we can avoid the semantic substitution lemma by cleverly stating the fundamental theorem.

Fundamental theorem attempt (de Bruijn env):
`has_type Δ Γ t T -> forall (δ : list rel) (ρ : atom -> rel) (γ : term env), ...`

Wait, the types in the judgment have FVars from Δ and BVars from nested binders. The typing judgment handles binders by opening with FVars. In the T_TAbs case, we have `has_type (X::Δ) Γ (open_tm_ty t (FVar X)) (open_ty T (FVar X))` for fresh X. The IH at this judgment would yield, for all δ, ρ, ...: relation on `tm_ty_subst σ1' (open_tm_ty t (FVar X))` at type `open_ty T (FVar X)`. But our goal is at type T with δ = R::δ' and terms `open_tm_ty (tm_ty_subst σ1 t) U1` (after applying the tabs to U1). We need the semantic substitution lemma: relating `⟦open_ty T (FVar X)⟧(ρ[X↦R])` with `⟦T⟧(R::δ')`. Since `open_ty T (FVar X)` and T differ only in bvar 0 vs FVar X, and ρ[X↦R] interprets FVar X as R, the semantic interpretations coincide if we set up δ' appropriately. This is intuitively a straightforward induction on T, but T has binders; we need to handle the correspondence between the fvar-opening at level 0 and the de Bruijn environment. Formally:

Lemma (semantic opening): For all types T (which may contain bvars), all X, δ, ρ, and R:
`C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R]) = C T (??? :: δ) ρ`? Hmm, actually open_ty only substitutes bvar 0 at depth 0 (under binders, k increments). The type T may have free bvars at indices 0..n. `open_ty T (FVar X)` replaces bvar 0 (at top level) with FVar X, and bvar i>0 stays bvar i. In de Bruijn environment terms: C T δ ρ interprets bvar i as δ(i). C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R]) interprets the replaced FVar X as R, and bvar i as δ(i). So the two are the same iff the environment δ has δ(0) = R in the second... but in the first, bvar i (i>0) is interpreted as δ(i), whereas in the second, bvar i is interpreted as δ(i) as well. So actually we need: `C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R])` equals `C T δ' ρ` where δ' is an environment with... In `C T δ' ρ`, bvar 0 is interpreted as head of δ'; but `open_ty T (FVar X)` has FVar X in place of bvar 0, interpreted as R. So we need δ'(0) = R. And for i>0, open_ty leaves bvar i, interpreted as δ(i) in the first and δ'(i) in the second; so δ'(i) = δ(i) for i>0. Thus δ' = R :: δ. So the lemma: `C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R]) (t1,t2) ↔ C T (R :: δ) ρ (t1,t2)` for X fresh w.r.t. T and ρ? Wait, if X occurs free in T (as FVar X), then open_ty doesn't touch those (FVar X stays FVar X), and in the first interpretation, ρ[X↦R] interprets that FVar X as R, whereas in the second, ρ (without extension) interprets it as ρ(X). So the lemma requires X not free in T, OR we can use ρ' = ρ with X ↦ R on both sides: `C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R]) ↔ C T (R::δ) (ρ[X↦R])`. In the second, FVar X (if it occurs in T) is interpreted as R, matching. So the lemma holds for all X when both sides use ρ[X↦R]! Because the only occurrence of FVar X that matters is either from the opening (interpreted as R under ρ[X↦R]) or from T's free vars (also R). But wait, there's a subtlety: if T already contains FVar X and we open bvar 0 with FVar X, then the opened type has FVar X in both places, both interpreted as R, consistent with δ(0)=R. So the equality holds for all X. 

So the lemma should be provable by induction on T, with de Bruijn environments. Let's state it precisely:

For all T, δ, ρ, X, R, t1, t2:
`C (open_ty T (Ty_FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R]) t1 t2 ↔ C T (R :: δ) ρ' t1 t2` where ρ' = ? In the first, free FVars Y≠X are interpreted by ρ[X↦R](Y) = ρ(Y); in the second by ρ'(Y). If ρ' = ρ (with X↦R as well), fine. Let's just use the same ρ'' = ρ[X↦R] on both sides: `C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ ρ'' t1 t2 ↔ C T (R::δ) ρ'' t1 t2`. Hmm, but in the second, the free FVar X gets ρ''(X)=R, while in the first, the opened occurrences also get R. Consistent. So the lemma seems plausible.

Actually, the semantic opening lemma in Option A is exactly about relating `C (open_ty T (FVar X)) δ (ρ[X↦R])` and `C T (R::δ) ρ` — and this lemma itself is provable by induction on T with Option A's definition, because the All case of both sides extends environments consistently. Goal: C (open_ty (Ty_All T') (FVar X)) δ ρ'' t1 t2 ↔ C (Ty_All T') (R::δ) ρ t1 t2. So my earlier semantic opening lemma should be about `open_ty T (FVar X)` = open_ty_rec 0 (FVar X) T generally. The correspondence with de Bruijn environments: In `open_ty T (FVar X)`, bvar 0 of T is replaced by FVar X; but at a position under one binder, the index refers differently. Generalized lemma: For all k, T, δ, ρ, X, R:
`C (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) δ (ρ[X↦R]) t1 t2 ↔ C T (??? ) ...`
What's the correspondence? open_ty_rec k replaces bvar k (relative to top) with FVar X; in de Bruijn environment terms, bvar k at top is δ(k) (if k < |δ|). `C (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) δ (ρ[X↦R]) t1 t2 ↔ C T (set_nth k R δ) (ρ[X↦R]) t1 t2`.

Wait, careful about the environment for the left side: the left type `open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T` may contain bvar indices; they should be interpreted by δ. The right type T has an extra FVar X inserted at position k... ```
Fixpoint C (T : ty) (δ : list Rel) (ρ : atom -> Rel) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => (* interpret δ(i) as a relation on *values*? *) ...

  end
```

Hmm, but there's a problem: the arrow/universal value relations need to ensure the values are actually values (e.g., tm_abs) and locally closed, otherwise the applications `tm_app v1 a1` might not step. We can define C to include that reduction produces a value and the value relation is defined appropriately. Let's define V (value relation) separately or have C include `value v1 /\ value v2`. So in the arrow case of the *value* relation. Since C t1 t2 := ∃ v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ Vrel T δ ρ v1 v2, we can define Vrel by recursion on T:

```
Fixpoint Vrel (T) δ ρ (v1 v2) :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => δ(i) v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar X => ρ X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      (exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1) /\ ... /\ 
      forall a1 a2, Vrel T1 δ ρ a1 a2 -> Crel T2 δ ρ (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)
  | Ty_All T' =>
      exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
      forall U1 U2 R, Crel T' (R::δ) ρ (open_tm_ty s1 U1) (open_tm_ty s2 U2)
  end
with Crel T δ ρ t1 t2 := exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ Vrel T δ ρ v1 v2.
```

Mutual Fixpoint on T. Coq requires the recursion to be structural; both Vrel and Crel recurse on subterms of T (T1, T2, T') — in the arrow case, Vrel calls Crel T2 which is a subterm; Crel calls Vrel T. Is mutual recursion accepted if Crel T calls Vrel T (same T)? Coq's guard checker for mutual fixpoints: recursive calls must be on a subterm of the corresponding argument; Crel T calling Vrel T with the same T should be fine for mutual recursion (Vrel is in the same mutual block; decreasing argument? It requires that recursive calls be on structurally smaller arguments or the same mutual function? Actually Coq allows mutual recursion where one function calls another with the same argument, as long as the "principal argument" decreases in recursive calls; the mutual fixpoint guard condition says: a recursive call to a function in the block must have a structurally smaller argument than the caller's decreasing argument... Hmm, calling Vrel T from Crel T with the same T: the argument is not smaller. Is that accepted? For nested/mutual fixpoints, Coq's subterm criterion: the call graph must have a decreasing argument. I recall that `Fixpoint even n := match n with 0 => true | S n' => odd n' end with odd n := match n with 0 => false | S n' => even n' end` is fine: odd calls even on n' (smaller). If Crel T calls Vrel T (same T), that's not decreasing... but it's not a recursive call to the *same* function; the guard condition requires that in a mutual block, recursive calls are on a subterm of the caller's argument, where the caller's argument is the decreasing argument of the enclosing fixpoint. Since Crel and Vrel both have T as the decreasing argument, calling Vrel T from Crel T has the same T — not strictly smaller. I think Coq rejects that ("recursive call ... not enough arguments" or "not structurally decreasing").

We can avoid by inlining: define Crel T t1 t2 := let ... — or define Crel as a `Definition` that unfolds to `exists v1 v2, ... Vrel T ...`, with Vrel a Fixpoint that recursively calls Crel (a Definition) at subterms. But then Vrel's recursion calls Crel T2 which calls Vrel T2 — the guard checker sees the call to Crel (a constant, not recursive) and then Crel's body contains a call to Vrel, but since Crel is a Definition (not a Fixpoint), the guard checker checks Vrel's body: Vrel calls Crel (opaque constant) — no recursive call visible. Then we define Crel after Vrel as a plain Definition. This works but Vrel's arrow case would call Crel which is defined *after* Vrel, impossible. Unless we define Crel before Vrel as a Definition using Vrel... circular.

Alternative: use a single Fixpoint where Crel includes the reduction and value check, and Vrel is expressed inside: define `C T δ ρ t1 t2 := exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ V T δ ρ v1 v2` where V is the value relation. We can define V as a Fixpoint where the arrow case's body uses C at T2 — C is defined in terms of V. So V and C are mutually recursive with a "same argument" call V T → C T2 → V T2. Since C T2 is a *definitional unfolding* at a subterm, maybe we can write it as a single Fixpoint using `let` or by inlining C in V's definition:

```
Fixpoint V T δ ρ v1 v2 :=
 match T with
 | Arrow T1 T2 => ... forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 -> 
     (exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2)
 ...
 end.
```

Here the arrow case inlines C T2's unfolding as `exists w1 w2, ... V T2 ...` — and all recursive calls are on strict subterms T1, T2. The universal case recurses on T' with env R::δ, a strict subterm. Great — a single structural Fixpoint works! The term relation C T δ ρ t1 t2 can then be defined as `exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ V T δ ρ v1 v2` after V is defined. And the arrow case in V uses the *inlined* term relation at T2 (with reduction), which is exactly `C T2 δ ρ (tm_app v1 a1) (tm_app v2 a2)` definitionally. 

So:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (δ : list (tm -> tm -> Prop)) (ρ : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => match nth_error δ i with Some R => R v1 v2 | None => False end
  | Ty_FVar X => ρ X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      (forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 ->
         exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2)
  | Ty_All T' =>
      exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
        forall U1 U2 R, 
          (exists w1 w2, open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\ open_tm_ty s2 U2 -->* w2 /\ V T' (R::δ) ρ w1 w2)
  end.
```

### Environments

Type context Δ : list atom (all the free type variables in scope).
Term context Γ : list (atom * ty).

The fundamental theorem: for all Δ Γ t T, `has_type Δ Γ t T ->` for all:
- `σ1 σ2 : atom -> ty` (type substitutions mapping type vars to closed types; only vars in Δ matter),
- `σ` coherence? Hmm, actually the type substitutions σ1,σ2 will be used to substitute into the term: `tm_ty_subst_env σ1 t` and `tm_ty_subst_env σ2 t`.
- `ρ : atom -> tm -> tm -> Prop` for the free type variables of Δ,
- `γ : atom -> tm` ... term environments for Γ, left and right (γ1,γ2), with relatedness conditions `∀ x T, lookup_context x Γ = Some T -> C (T under ???) ...`.

Wait, the type context for the relation: types in Γ may mention type variables from Δ, so their semantic interpretation uses ρ (and δ=[]?). The fundamental theorem is at source types with free fvars only (no bvars at the judgment level? The type T in `has_type Δ Γ t T` can contain fvars from Δ but no free bvars — typing rules open binders, so at the top of the judgment, types have no free bvars. Yes! The typing judgment only ever deals with types with free fvars (from Δ) and no free de Bruijn indices, because binders are opened. Great, so in the fundamental theorem, δ = [] for T and for the types in Γ. And D is a de Bruijn environment needed only when we go under a universal; but the All case of the semantics uses T' with bvar 0. In the fundamental theorem at T = Ty_All T', we need to show the universal relation, which recurses into T' with δ = R :: []. And we connect to the IH at `open_ty T' (FVar X)` with ρ[X↦R] via the semantic opening lemma with k=0, δ=[]... but the semantic opening lemma's LHS environment is δ = [] and it needs `C (open_ty_rec 0 (FVar X) T') [] ρ' ≡ C T' (replace_nth 0 (ρ' X) []) ρ'`. `replace_nth 0 R []` should be `[R]`. So we need `replace_nth` to extend if out of range. Hmm, earlier I thought out-of-range would fail because the left type has the bvar replaced by FVar X (interpreted as R) while the right type's bvar might be beyond δ. But if `replace_nth k R δ` *extends* δ to length k+1 (padding with a default), then the right side interprets bvar k as R, matching. And other out-of-range indices i > k? Left: bvar i remains, interpreted by default (False); right: extended δ padded; if i is beyond the extension... Let's define the de Bruijn environment as an arbitrary function `nat -> Rel` rather than a list! Then there's no out-of-range issue. δ : nat -> (tm -> tm -> Prop). The All case: `C T' (fun 0 => R, fun (S n) => δ n)` or `δ' = update δ 0 R`. Then the semantic opening lemma:

`C (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) δ ρ t1 t2 ↔ C T (fun i => if i =? k then ρ X else δ i) ρ t1 t2`.

Let's check the All case: LHS `open_ty_rec k (FVar X) (Ty_All T') = Ty_All (open_ty_rec (k+1) (FVar X) T')`. Unfold: for all R_sem, C (open_ty_rec (k+1) (FVar X) T') (shift δ with 0↦R_sem) ρ ... RHS `C (Ty_All T') δ_k ρ` unfolds: for all R_sem, C T' (shift δ_k with 0↦R_sem) ρ. By IH with k+1: `open_ty_rec (k+1) ...` under δ1 = shift(δ, R_sem) corresponds to `T'` under δ1[k+1 ↦ ρ X] = shift(δ_k, R_sem)? Let's compute: δ1(i) = if i=0 then R_sem else δ(i-1). δ1[k+1↦ρX](i) = if i=k+1 then ρX else δ1(i). shift(δ_k, R_sem)(i) = if i=0 then R_sem else δ_k(i-1) = if i=0 then R_sem else (if i-1=k then ρX else δ(i-1)) = if i=0 then R_sem elsif i=k+1 then ρX else δ(i-1). Equal. Great. So with function environments, the semantic opening lemma holds for all k without range issues, and we can state:

```
Lemma V_open_fvar : forall T k X ρ R δ v1 v2,
  V (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) δ ρ v1 v2 <->
  V T (fun i => if Nat.eqb i k then R else δ i) (fun Y => if Nat.eqb Y X then R else ρ Y) v1 v2.
```

Wait, I need to be careful with ρ: on the LHS, the type has FVar X inserted, and other fvars Y interpreted by ρ. The inserted FVar X is interpreted by ρ(X). For the equivalence with the RHS where T's bvar k is interpreted by R, we should have ρ(X) = R? Actually, on the LHS, if T also contains free occurrences of FVar X, they're interpreted by ρ(X) as well. The RHS has T with its FVar X interpreted by ρ(X). So if we use the same ρ on both sides, then the inserted FVar X is interpreted as ρ(X), while the RHS's bvar k is interpreted by δ(k). For the equivalence we need δ(k) = ρ(X). So the statement should be: `V T (update_at k (ρ X) δ) ρ`. I earlier had R as a separate variable with ρ' = ρ[X↦R]; let's just set R = ρ(X) and use the same ρ. So:

```
Lemma V_open_fvar : forall T k X δ ρ v1 v2,

Let me now write the precise statement.

Definitions:

```
Definition rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.
Definition tyenv := nat -> rel.  (* de Bruijn env for bound type vars *)
Definition fvtyenv := atom -> rel.
Definition tmenv := atom -> tm.
```

Semantics:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (δ : nat -> rel) (ρ : atom -> rel) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => δ i v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar X => ρ X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 ->
         exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2)
  | Ty_All T' =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
        forall U1 U2, locally_closed_ty U1 -> locally_closed_ty U2 ->
          forall R, 
          (exists w1 w2, open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\ open_tm_ty s2 U2 -->* w2 /\ V T' (extend_rel R δ) ρ w1 w2)
  end.
```

Wait: the arrow case needs v1 to be a tm_abs to guarantee the application steps. `value v1` could be tm_tabs, which for arrow type is wrong. In the fundamental theorem, well-typed closed terms of arrow type reduce to tm_abs values, so the relation V at arrow will require more. Let's add shape: `(exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1)`. Hmm, but the lc condition for ST_AppAbs requires `locally_closed_tm (tm_abs T1' b1)`. Since `value v1` gives `locally_closed_tm v1` via the value constructors: value (tm_abs T t) is only constructed with lc. But to get the lc out we destruct `value v1`; if v1 = tm_abs T1' b1 and value v1, then v_abs case gives lc. So we should require shape + value. Let me define:

Arrow case:
```
exists b1 b2, v1 = tm_abs T?? b1 ...
```
Hmm, we don't need the annotation, just the body. But ST_AppAbs requires lc of `tm_abs T t`; value v1 gives lc of v1; if v1 = tm_abs T' b then lc (tm_abs T' b) by injection. So:

```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
    value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    (exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1) /\
    (exists T2' b2, v2 = tm_abs T2' b2) /\
    forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 -> exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2
```

Universal case:
```
| Ty_All T' =>
    value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
      forall U1 U2, locally_closed_ty U1 -> locally_closed_ty U2 -> forall R,

We could also require `locally_closed_tm (tm_tabs s1)` directly. Let's see what's convenient.

Term relation:

```
Definition C T δ ρ t1 t2 :=
  exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ V T δ ρ v1 v2.
```

### Fundamental theorem

Statement:

`has_type Δ Γ t T -> forall σ1 σ2 ρ γ1 γ2, (forall x Tx, lookup_context x Γ = Some Tx -> C Tx zero δ? ρ (γ1 x) (γ2 x)) -> C T zero ρ (tm_ty_subst_env σ1 (tm_subst_env γ1 t)) (tm_ty_subst_env σ2 (tm_subst_env γ2 t))`

Hmm wait — the type substitution σ should be applied both to the term and... the semantic interpretation of T uses ρ for free type vars, and σ maps those vars to types. In the T_Var case: `lookup_context x Γ = Some Tx`, conclusion `C Tx zero ρ (subst σ1 (γ1 x?))...`. Hmm, careful: the term `tm_subst_env γ t` substitutes fvar x with γ(x); if t = tm_fvar x then it's γ(x). Then `tm_ty_subst_env σ (γ1 x)` — the substituted value also gets type-substituted? Let me think about the role of σ: the theorem is about terms with free type variables from Δ. We substitute them with closed types to obtain closed terms. In the locally nameless representation, `tm_ty_subst_env σ t` replaces FVar type annotations and type arguments. For the T_Var case, t = tm_fvar x, and the conclusion is about `tm_ty_subst_env σ (tm_subst_env γ (tm_fvar x)) = tm_ty_subst_env σ (γ x)`. So the environment values also get type-substituted. The environment hypothesis should then be about `C Tx zero ρ (tm_ty_subst_env σ1 (γ1 x)) (tm_ty_subst_env σ2 (γ2 x))`. Hmm, but there's a subtlety: γ is a term substitution for *all* fvars, but under term binders, the environment gets extended. Standard.

Wait, but actually, is it necessary to apply σ to the term at all? The term t has free type variables from Δ possibly. In the fundamental theorem we want to conclude something about closed terms eventually. However, there's a mismatch: in the T_TAbs case, the IH is applied to `open_tm_ty s (FVar X)` under context (X::Δ). The term substitution applied is `tm_ty_subst_env σ'` where σ'(X)=U1. Similarly for the term variable environment: T_Abs opens the body with `tm_fvar x`, and the IH is applied under Γ' = (x,T1)::Γ. The term substitution γ' extends γ with γ'(x) = the argument value. If γ'(x) = γ(x), fine. Since we'll extend γ at x with a new value, the environment's old value at x might be anything, but we can define γ' = fun y => if y = x then new else γ y. Then for free FVar x in t (which shouldn't occur if x is fresh for the terms in the environment and derivation... in locally nameless, the binder name x is chosen fresh w.r.t. L which includes free variables of the context and term, so FVar x doesn't occur free in t? Hmm, x is fresh w.r.t. L, and L from the typing rule includes the domain of Γ and free variables of the term? This is a lot of syntactic lemma work: substitution lemmas for open_tm, tm_subst_env, open_tm_ty, tm_ty_subst_env. But these are standard and can be proven by induction. The volume is significant but manageable.

### Environment condition and T_Abs case

Let's think through the T_Abs case to make sure the semantics works with argument values.

T_Abs: t = tm_abs T1 t2, T = Ty_Arrow T1 T2. Premise: wf_ty Δ T1 and forall x ∉ L, has_type Δ (x:T1 :: Γ) (open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)) T2.

Goal: C (Ty_Arrow T1 T2) zero ρ (subst σ1 (subst_tm γ1 (tm_abs T1 t2))) (subst σ2 (...)).
Note: tm_subst_env γ (tm_abs T1 t2) = tm_abs (ty_subst_env? no—) Hmm! `tm_subst` for tm_abs keeps T unchanged: `tm_abs T (tm_subst x s t1)`. And `tm_ty_subst` changes T. So `tm_ty_subst_env σ (tm_subst_env γ (tm_abs T1 t2))` = `tm_abs (ty_subst_env σ T1) (tm_ty_subst_env σ (tm_subst_env γ t2))`.

C means: exists v1 v2, the substituted term -->* v1, etc., V at arrow. The substituted term is `tm_abs ...`. It's a value if locally closed. We can take v1 = the term itself, reduction refl, provided it's a value (needs locally_closed_tm). Then V at arrow requires:
- value v1, value v2 (need lc of the substituted abs, which requires lc_ty of the annotation and lc_tm of body under binder).
- shape: v1 = tm_abs ... trivially.
- for all a1 a2, V T1 zero ρ a1 a2 -> exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 ∧ ... V T2 ... w1 w2.

Given V T1 zero ρ a1 a2, we know a1,a2 are values (from V's definition: we should make V at any type imply value? For T1 arbitrary, V T1 could be e.g. Ty_BVar i with δ i that doesn't require values; but at the top level δ=zero, and T1 has no free bvars, so T1 is fvar or arrow or all; V at fvar is ρ X which is an arbitrary relation on values... we might need ρ X to imply values? In the final instantiation, ρ X = {(v,v)}, and v is a value. In general, for the environment, ρ can be arbitrary; but the T_Var case and the arrow argument case need V at T1 to imply value/lc for the application to step. Hmm.

Let's examine: in the T_Abs case, the goal relation at arrow needs `tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1`. v1 = tm_abs T1' body with lc. To step via ST_AppAbs, need `value a1` and lc of the abs. `V T1 zero ρ a1 a2` where T1 is an arbitrary type from the source program. Standard parametricity for CBV: the relation at type variables is a relation on *values* (closed values), so ρ(X) a1 a2 already implies a1,a2 are values (and closed). And for the T_Var case, the environment hypothesis similarly must give related values at Tx after σ substitution; we can require the term environment to satisfy `∀ x Tx, lookup ... -> C Tx zero ρ (subst σ1 (γ1 x)) (subst σ2 (γ2 x))` and we need those to be values for application? Hmm, at T_Var, the goal is C Tx ... of the substituted variable term; the environment hypothesis gives exactly that. At top-level judgments, T1 has no free bvars, so no. Under the universal's body, the type may contain bvars: in the universal case, the type T' under δ = R::δ'. Then V T1 with T1 = Ty_BVar 0 gives R a1 a2, and R is an arbitrary relation provided by the universal quantifier. To apply the arrow case inside, we need R to relate values. So the environment of relations ρ must satisfy:
`well_rel ρ := forall X v1 v2, ρ X v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2 /\ locally_closed_tm v1 /\ locally_closed_tm v2`.
Actually `value` already implies locally_closed_tm by the constructors (value (tm_abs T t) requires lc). Let's check: `value` inductive has constructors v_abs (lc premise) and v_tabs (lc premise). Let me step back and think about how this is normally done in "logical relations for System F" proofs, e.g., in the Software Foundations / TAPL chapter or the "free theorems" literature. The standard fundamental theorem for System F (e.g., in Reynolds' parametricity or in "Theorems for Free" mechanizations) typically uses *sets of values* and interprets types by semantic types; type variables range over arbitrary relations; and terms are related under type substitutions. The issue with CBV and substitutions is usually handled by defining the relation on *closed terms* and using the substitution lemma as part of the metatheory: when relating `(λx.t) a` to `(λx.t') a'`, the step goes to `t[a]`, and the IH applies at the environment. The key point is that in the standard setup, the two sides are different terms/type substitutions, and the environment maps x to the pair (a,a'), NOT to the type-substituted terms. The IH is stated as: for all environments γ mapping x to related pairs (a,a') (which are already the terms we'll use), the conclusion relates `t[γ]` to `t'[γ']`. There's no σ applied to γ values because the σ substitution is applied to the whole term t and the environment values are the actual values used. Wait, in our setup, why did σ get applied to γ values? Because we defined `subst σ (subst γ t)`; naturally, the substitution order is: first close type variables (σ), then term variables (γ)? The environment values γ(x) are used as the actual arguments, so they shouldn't be type-substituted. Let's instead define the *closing* as `tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t)`: first substitute types in t, then substitute term variables with γ values. Then the T_Var case: `tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ (tm_fvar x)) = γ x` — no σ on γ x! And the T_Abs case: `tm_subst_env γ' (tm_ty_subst_env σ (open_tm t2 (fvar x)))`. By a commutation lemma, this equals `open_tm (tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t2)) (γ' x)`? Hmm, we need to commute term substitution with the type substitution and the term opening: `subst_env γ' (subst_ty σ (open_tm t2 (fvar x)))`. Since subst_ty σ and open commute (as we discussed), this is `subst_env γ' (open_tm (subst_ty σ t2) (fvar X?))` — wait, open_tm t2 (fvar x): x is a term fvar, and subst_ty doesn't touch term fvars, but it does substitute type fvars in annotations. `subst_ty σ (open_tm t2 (tm_fvar x)) = open_tm (subst_ty σ t2) (tm_fvar x)` since the opened argument is a term fvar unaffected by type substitution. Then `subst_env γ' (open_tm X (fvar x)) = open_tm (subst_env γ' X) (γ' x) = open_tm (subst_env γ X) (γ' x)` (if x not free in X). And the step ST_AppAbs gives `open_tm (subst_env γ (subst_ty σ t2)) a1` where γ' x = a1. Match! 

So the fix is to substitute types BEFORE term variables: `tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t)`. Then the environment values are not type-substituted, and the T_Var case gives γ x directly matching the environment condition. Excellent. This is a crucial correction.

But hmm, is it semantically fine that the environment values γ x are not type-substituted? The environment condition says C Tx zero ρ (γ1 x) (γ2 x), where Tx is the type in Γ (containing fvars from Δ). The actual terms γ x are just values, and they're related at Tx under ρ. The type substitution σ is applied to the term but not to the environment values. Since σ only affects the source term's annotations, that's coherent: we're substituting types in the term's syntax while the environment values are held fixed. If lc_tm_at 0 0 (open_tm_rec 0 (fvar x) t2), we want lc_tm_at 0 1 t2. Intuitively, bvar 0 in t2 becomes the fvar x, which is lc; and other bvars j≥1 remain and must be lc in the opened term at depth... In the opened term, at the top, bvar j (j≥1) is still bvar j; lc_tm_at 0 0 allows no bvars, so no j≥1 at top. Under binders inside t2, the lc_tm_at depth check shifts: e.g., t2 = tm_abs T (tm_bvar 1) (the body's bvar 1 refers to... in t2 = tm_abs T body, bvar 0 in body is bound by tm_abs; bvar 1 would be bound by the outer tm_abs of the original term, i.e., open_tm_rec 0 u (tm_abs T body) leaves body's bvar 0 (bound) and substitutes bvar 1 with u. Thus lc_tm_at 0 0 of the opened term (tm_abs T body') requires lc_tm_at 0 1 body'; if u is lc, then body's lc follows appropriately. The general inversion lemma is non-trivial but standard.

Alternatively, avoid proving source lc by proving lc of the *closed* substituted terms directly from typing + substitution. Same difficulty essentially.

Hmm, hold on. Maybe we can define the semantics over an untyped "raw" step relation that ignores lc premises, then show that the actual `step` relation's lc premises are satisfied using a separate lc analysis of the specific target computation. But the target computation is exactly the semantic one; we'd still need lc of terms.

Let me think about the lc problem differently: we can add the lc conditions as *assumptions in the semantic relation* and derive them in the fundamental theorem from the typing derivation via a tailored lc lemma. The lc lemma's proof needs open inversion. Let's just do the standard locally nameless metatheory. There are well-known lemma sets. Let me enumerate them carefully.

### Locally nameless lemmas needed

Definitions:
```

fv_ty T? maybe not needed if we use substitution congruence

```

Standard lemmas (from TAPL/Software Foundations "Stlc_lt" etc.):
1. `open_ty_rec_lc`: if `lc_ty_at k U` and `lc_ty_at K T` where k < K? Let's derive: `lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T)` under conditions. Opening replaces bvar k with U; so we need U lc at K (same K), and T lc at K with the bvar k possibly free (it's allowed since k < K). Then result lc at K. But there's a subtlety: in T, bvars < K; bvar k is replaced by U; others stay. So if lc_ty_at K T and lc_ty_at K U, then lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T). Wait, under Ty_All, open_ty_rec increments k, and lc_ty_at decrements K (body lc at S K from All lc at K). So with k and K both incremented, the condition k < K is preserved? If k=K-1, under the binder it becomes k+1 = K and the body's K is K+1, so k+1 < K+1 ✓. So we need k < K as a hypothesis for bvar k to be allowed in T. Actually if k ≥ K, T can't contain bvar k (lc), so opening does nothing at that index; the lemma still holds without k<K? If T contains no bvar k, then open_ty_rec k U T = T (structurally, since the only changes are at bvars = k). Then lc holds from T's lc; U's lc not needed. So the lemma can be stated: lc_ty_at K T -> lc_ty_at K U -> lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T). But under binders, K increases and k increases; U is fixed and lc at K; need lc at S K, which follows by weakening. So we'd use monotonicity. Alternatively state: `lc_ty_at K T -> (k < K -> lc_ty_at K U) -> lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T)`.

Hmm, but in our uses, U may contain fvars and be lc at 0; then lc at any K by weakening.

2. `lc_ty_at_weaken`: k ≤ k' -> lc_ty_at k T -> lc_ty_at k' T. (bvars < k ≤ k').

3. `open_ty_rec_inv`: lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T) -> lc_ty_at K T? Not exactly; the opened type has U where bvar k was; we want to invert to get T's lc. If U is lc at K and k < K? Hmm: Let's see. If k < K, then bvar k is allowed in lc_ty_at K T. So actually `lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T) -> k < K -> lc_ty_at K T`? So `lc_ty_at K (open_ty_rec k U T) -> k < K -> lc_ty_at K U -> lc_ty_at K T`. Hmm, do we need U lc? Inside, the opened body at depth S K contains U, which is lc at S K; to reconstruct T's body lc, U's lc isn't needed for T... but the induction hypothesis may require it. So we can't derive U lc in general. Then the lc preservation under substitution: `lc_tm_at 0 0 t -> (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ X)) -> (forall x, locally_closed_tm (γ x)) -> locally_closed_tm (tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t))`. Hmm, is this exactly true? lc_tm_at 0 0 t means no free bvars; but t may contain fvars of both kinds (term and type). Type substitution with lc types maps type fvars to lc types; but wait, σ maps every atom to a type; if σ X contains free type *fvars* that's fine (fvars allowed in lc_ty_at); if it contains bvars, lc fails. So require `forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ X)`. Term substitution γ with lc terms similarly. But there's a subtlety: type binders inside t: tm_tabs t1 has t1 with K=1; when we substitute type fvars in annotations... the bvars of t remain bound. σ only substitutes fvars, so lc bvar structure is preserved. Term substitution similarly. Should be provable by induction on t with a generalized statement for open? Let's see: for t with lc_tm_at 0 0, direct induction on t:
- tm_bvar i: lc gives i<0 impossible; contradiction. Hmm, lc_tm_at 0 0 t at top has no bvars, but under binders there are bvars. So the induction needs a generalized statement: for `lc_tm_at K k t`, show `lc_tm_at K k (close σ γ t)` provided σ maps to lc types at K (weaken) and γ maps to lc terms at K,k? Since γ values are lc at 0,0, they're lc at any K,k by weakening. Let's state: `lc_tm_at K k t -> (forall X, lc_ty_at K (σ X)) -> (forall x, lc_tm_at K k (γ x)) -> lc_tm_at K k (tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t))`. Hmm, wait: tm_subst_env γ substitutes term fvars with γ x; the result must have the same bvar indices; since γ x is lc at K,k (no bvars < ... it's closed), fine. Type substitution: annotations; lc_ty_at K of σ X. Under a type binder, K increments, and the annotations may contain fvars substituted by σ which is lc at K hence at S K by weakening. Fine. But note: the type substitution function `ty_subst_env σ T` replaces FVars with σ X. The lc proof needs: lc_ty_at K T -> (forall X, lc_ty_at K (σ X)) -> lc_ty_at K (ty_subst_env σ T). Provable by induction on T. Under All: K increments; σ needs lc at S K, use weakening. ✓.

Also we need lc of the specific *abs* in the T_Abs case: `value (tm_abs (ty_subst_env σ T1) (close σ γ t2))`, requiring lc_tm_at 0 0 (tm_abs ...) = lc_ty_at 0 (ty_subst_env σ T1) ∧ lc_tm_at 0 1 (close σ γ t2). The first from lc_ty_at 0 T1 (wf) and σ lc. The second: from lc_tm_at 0 1 t2 (from has_type lc) and substitution preservation generalized at K=0,k=1. But the γ and σ hypotheses at K=0,k=1: σ lc at 0 → lc at any K; γ lc at 0,0 → lc at 0,1. ✓.

Also in other places (V arrow), when we need lc of the *argument* terms a1,a2 for ST_AppAbs, we get value a_i from the semantics. Where does the semantics get value a_i? The V relation at arrow gives related arguments' arguments; let's define the semantic relation such that V implies value (a lemma proven by induction on the type). For T1 = fvar/bvar, we rely on the value-relatedness of ρ/δ. So we need to carry a well-formedness hypothesis on ρ (and δ) throughout: 
`wf_rel ρ := forall X v1 v2, ρ X v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2`
and for δ: `wf_rel_delta δ := forall i v1 v2, δ i v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2`.
Actually, the de Bruijn δ entries arise only from the universal relation's quantified R, which we can require to be value-related. Let's design V_All to quantify over value-related R only:
```
forall U1 U2, lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
forall R, (forall v1 v2, R v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2) ->
  exists w1 w2, open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\ open_tm_ty s2 U2 -->* w2 /\ V T' (extend R δ) ρ w1 w2
```
Then the lemma `V T δ ρ v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2` needs the hypotheses wf_rel ρ and wf_rel_delta δ. But hold on: do we need `value` or do we need *shape* (tm_abs/tm_tabs)? In the arrow case, we need to know v1 is a tm_abs to apply ST_AppAbs; `value v1` plus the arrow case's shape requirement. We also need the relations at fvar/bvar to give values. And for the argument's application, the arrow relation at T1 must give value a1; the lemma provides it.

Also, for the universal case, applying `open_tm_ty s1 U1` requires `locally_closed_tm (tm_tabs s1)` (which together with shape gives lc of the tabs) and `lc_ty_at 0 U1`. We require lc_ty U1,U2 in the quantifier. The lc of `tm_tabs s1` we get from `value v1` (since v1 = tm_tabs s1) or from the V definition requiring value. So the V_All case: `value v1 ∧ value v2 ∧ exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 ∧ v2 = tm_tabs s2 ∧ ...`.

Now, an important question: is the value-relatedness of ρ preserved in the T_TAbs case when we extend ρ with R? The IH requires wf_rel ρ' where ρ' = ρ[X↦R]; since R is value-related and ρ is, ρ' is. ✓. And in the fundamental theorem, we require wf_rel ρ as a hypothesis.

Also δ in the fundamental theorem: at the top, δ = fun _ => False? Actually the fundamental theorem's types have no free bvars, so the δ environment is irrelevant. We can set δ = fun _ v1 v2 => False (empty relation), which is value-related. But in the V_All case, the body relation uses `extend R δ`. In the semantic opening lemma, we relate `V (open_ty T (FVar X)) zero ...` — hmm, here the LHS type `open_ty T (FVar X)` may contain bvars under nested binders but those get bound by V's recursion; at the top it has no free bvars, so δ = zero irrelevant. And the RHS `V T (extend R zero) ...` has bvar 0 interpreted as R. The semantic opening lemma with k=0: `V (open_ty_rec 0 (FVar X) T) δ ρ ≡ V T (replace_nth 0 (ρ X) δ) ρ`. With δ = zero = fun _ => False, `replace_nth 0 R zero` = extend R zero. ✓ (where extend R δ := fun i => match i with 0 => R | S j => δ j end).

Wait, the lemma statement uses `ρ X` as the replacement, and in the application ρ is ρ' = ρ[X↦R] so ρ'(X)=R. ✓.

Now, what about the fundamental theorem's δ? The judgment types may have bvars? `has_type Δ Γ t T` — T can have free bvars? The typing rules only manipulate types via opening with fvars and substituting; all judgment types are "locally closed" in the sense that they have no free bvars (binders internal). I believe so: T_Var: T from Γ, which is a type stored in the context — could it have free bvars? Γ is constructed from T_Abs with annotation T1 (which comes from the source abs annotation, wf_ty Δ T1 — no free bvars). T_TApp: open_ty T U. T_TAbs: Ty_All T. So yes, no free bvars in judgment types. But the fundamental theorem can just use δ = zero and never expose it: V T zero ρ. The All case extends with R. For a type T that has no free bvars, the δ entries are never consulted except through binders. ✓.

Now let's nail the definition of V and C and the lemmas:

```
Definition rel := tm -> tm -> Prop.
Definition ty_fenv := atom -> rel.
Definition ty_benv := nat -> rel.

Definition extend_b (R : rel) (δ : ty_benv) : ty_benv :=
  fun i => match i with 0 => R | S j => δ j end.

Fixpoint V (T : ty) (δ : ty_benv) (ρ : ty_fenv) (v1 v2 : tm) : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => δ i v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar X => ρ X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (exists T1' t1', v1 = tm_abs T1' t1') /\
      (exists T2' t2', v2 = tm_abs T2' t2') /\
      (forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 ->
         exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2)
  | Ty_All T' =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (exists s1, v1 = tm_tabs s1) /\
      (exists s2, v2 = tm_tabs s2) /\
      (forall U1 U2, lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        forall R, (forall x1 x2, R x1 x2 -> value x1 /\ value x2) ->
        exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
          exists w1 w2,
            open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\
            open_tm_ty s2 U2 -->* w2 /\
            V T' (extend_b R δ) ρ w1 w2)
  end.
```

Hmm, the exists s1 s2 with v1 = tm_tabs s1 etc. appears both outside and inside the forall. Simplify: put the exists outside:
```
| Ty_All T' =>
    value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
      forall U1 U2, lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        forall R, (forall x1 x2, R x1 x2 -> value x1 /\ value x2) ->
          exists w1 w2, open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\ ... /\ V T' (extend_b R δ) ρ w1 w2
```

Similarly arrow:
```
| Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
    value v1 /\ value v2 /\
    (exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1) /\
    (exists T2' b2, v2 = tm_abs T2' b2) /\
    forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 -> exists w1 w2, ... 
```

Note: when using V arrow, we can get `v1 = tm_abs ...` and `value v1`; value + equality gives lc of the abs via inversion of value. We need a lemma `value_inv_abs : value (tm_abs T b) -> locally_closed_tm (tm_abs T b)` — actually `value (tm_abs T b)` has constructor v_abs with lc premise, so inversion gives it directly.

But hmm: V is defined with `value v1` and `exists ... v1 = tm_abs ...`. From `value v1` and `v1 = tm_abs T' b`, we can rewrite to get `value (tm_abs T' b)` and invert. Fine.

Also, `value` in the arrow case: do we need `value v2` too? Symmetric, yes.

Term relation:
```
Definition C (T : ty) (δ : ty_benv) (ρ : ty_fenv) (t1 t2 : tm) : Prop :=
  exists v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 /\ t2 -->* v2 /\ V T δ ρ v1 v2.
```

Semantic opening lemma:

```
Lemma V_open_ty_rec : forall T k (X : atom) (δ : ty_benv) (ρ : ty_fenv) v1 v2,
  V (open_ty_rec k (Ty_FVar X) T) δ ρ v1 v2 <->
  V T (fun i => if Nat.eqb i k then ρ X else δ i) ρ v1 v2.
```
Wait, earlier I noted the same ρ on both sides with the replacement using ρ X. Let me re-derive with this V definition including the All case quantifying lc types and value-related R: In the All case, LHS is `V (Ty_All (open_ty_rec (k+1) (FVar X) T')) δ ρ`; RHS is `V (Ty_All T') (replace k (ρ X) δ) ρ`. Both unfold to quantification over U1,U2 lc and value-related R, and then recurse with δ' := extend_b R δ vs extend_b R (replace k (ρX) δ). By IH with k+1 and environment δ' (or the right side). We computed: extend_b R (replace_at k (ρX) δ) = replace_at (k+1) (ρX) (extend_b R δ). ✓ by definition if replace_at is defined as `fun i => if i =? k then R else δ i`.

Wait, careful: The LHS's body type is `open_ty_rec (k+1) (FVar X) T'` and the environment is `extend_b R_l δ`. The RHS's body type is T' with environment `extend_b R_l (replace_at k (ρX) δ)`. By IH (with k+1, environment extend_b R_l δ): LHS body V ↔ `V T' (replace_at (k+1) (ρX) (extend_b R_l δ)) ρ`. And we need this to equal `extend_b R_l (replace_at k (ρX) δ)`. Check: replace_at (k+1) (ρX) (extend_b R_l δ) i = if i =? (k+1) then ρX else (extend_b R_l δ) i = if i =? k+1 then ρX else (if i =? 0 then R_l else δ (i-1)). extend_b R_l (replace_at k (ρX) δ) i = if i =? 0 then R_l else (replace_at k (ρX) δ) (i-1) = if i =? 0 then R_l else (if i-1 =? k then ρX else δ (i-1)). For i=0: LHS: 0 =? k+1 false → extend_b R_l δ 0 = R_l ✓ RHS R_l. For i=S j: LHS: S j =? k+1 ↔ j =? k; if true ρX else δ j; RHS: S j =? 0 false → if j =? k then ρX else δ j ✓. Great, need `Nat.eqb (S j) (S k) = Nat.eqb j k`, and `Nat.eqb (S j) 0 = false`, both by computation/simpl. So the lemma is provable by induction on T, with the All case needing this arithmetic simplification and the fact that V_All's quantifiers match. Note: the lemma statement uses the same ρ; the quantifier over R with value-relatedness is identical. ✓.

Now the free-variable congruence lemma for V:

```
Fixpoint fv_ty (T : ty) : list atom := ...
```
Or simpler: since we only need to change ρ at a fresh variable X not occurring in T, we can prove:
`Lemma V_agree : forall T δ ρ1 ρ2, (forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> ρ1 X = ρ2 X) -> V T δ ρ1 v1 v2 <-> V T δ ρ2 v1 v2`.
But maybe simpler to state with a single variable: `X not free in T -> V T δ (upd ρ X R) v1 v2 ↔ V T δ ρ v1 v2` for all R... Careful: if X not free in T, then both sides are the same; provable by induction on T using a `fv_ty` predicate. We'll need `fv_ty` for this and possibly for freshness. Let's define:

```
Fixpoint fv_ty (T : ty) : list atom :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => []
  | Ty_FVar X => [X]
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 => fv_ty T1 ++ fv_ty T2
  | Ty_All T => fv_ty T
  end.
```
Note: bvars have no fvars; All's body fvars include the free ones (bound bvar 0 isn't an fvar). Good.

Then:
`Lemma V_fv_agree : forall T δ ρ1 ρ2, (forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> ρ1 X = ρ2 X) -> forall v1 v2, V T δ ρ1 v1 v2 <-> V T δ ρ2 v1 v2`.
Proof induction on T; All case: body T' has fv_ty T'; the quantifiers over R match; apply IH. ✓.

And the special case: `~ In X (fv_ty T) -> V T δ (fun Y => if Y =? X then R else ρ Y) v1 v2 ↔ V T δ ρ v1 v2`. This follows from V_fv_agree since for Y ∈ fv_ty T, Y ≠ X so ρ'(Y)=ρ(Y).

Hmm, I realize we also need a similar lemma for `C` but it follows by unfolding C.

Now the fundamental theorem. Let's write the exact statement.

```
Definition close (σ : atom -> ty) (γ : atom -> tm) (t : tm) : tm :=
  tm_subst_env γ (tm_ty_subst_env σ t).
```

Fundamental theorem:

```
Theorem fundamental : forall Δ Γ t T,
  has_type Δ Γ t T ->
  forall σ1 σ2 δ ρ γ1 γ2,
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ1 X)) ->
    (forall X, lc_ty_at 0 (σ2 X)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed_tm (γ1 x)) ->
    (forall x, locally_closed_tm (γ2 x)) ->
    (forall X v1 v2, ρ X v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2) ->
    (forall x Tx, lookup_context x Γ = Some Tx -> C Tx δ ρ (γ1 x) (γ2 x)) ->
    C T δ ρ (close σ1 γ1 t) (close σ2 γ2 t).
```

   Then tm_subst_env γ' (open_tm X (tm_fvar x)) with X = tm_ty_subst_env σ t2. General lemma: `tm_subst_env γ (open_tm_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_tm_rec k (γ x) (tm_subst_env γ t)` provided γ maps to lc terms. Let's derive with k=0. As earlier, under tm_abs the index increments and the RHS opens the substituted body with γ x at index S k; LHS substitutes γ' into open_rec (S k) (fvar x); by induction generalized to k. The condition: γ values are locally closed (no free bvars) so that substituting into the opened argument is fine. Let's carefully prove:
   
   Claim: for all k, γ, x, t: if (forall y, lc_tm_at 0 0 (γ y)) — actually we need no free bvars in γ y at any depth, which locally_closed_tm gives — then `tm_subst_env γ (open_tm_rec k (tm_fvar x) t) = open_tm_rec k (γ x) (tm_subst_env γ t)`.
   Proof by induction on t:
   - tm_bvar i: LHS: open_tm_rec k (fvar x) (tm_bvar i) = if i =? k then fvar x else bvar i; then subst = if i=?k then γ x else bvar i. RHS: subst γ (bvar i) = bvar i; open_rec k (γ x) (bvar i) = if i=?k then γ x else bvar i. Same ✓.
   - tm_fvar y: LHS: open (fvar y) = fvar y (open doesn't touch fvars); subst = γ y. RHS: subst = γ y; open_rec k (γ x) (γ y) = γ y since γ y is lc (no bvars) — open_tm_rec only substitutes bvar k; lc means no free bvars, so unchanged. Need a lemma `lc_tm_at 0 0 u -> open_tm_rec k v u = u`. Provable by induction on u. ✓

`tm_ty_subst_env σ' (open_tm_ty_rec k U t) = open_tm_ty_rec k (ty_subst_env σ' U) (tm_ty_subst_env σ t)` where σ' agrees with σ on fvars? Since the operations commute except at the inserted U/type bvars, and bvars are not fvars, RHS's open replaces bvar k with ty_subst_env σ U, matching. So this holds for *any* σ, no freshness needed! ```
Fixpoint fv_tm_ty (t : tm) : list atom :=
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar _ => []
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv_ty T ++ fv_tm_ty t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_tm_ty t2

  | tm_tapp t1 T => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_ty T
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => fv_tm_ty t1 ++ fv_tm_ty t2
  end.
```

```
Fixpoint fv_tm (t : tm) : list atom :=  (* free term variables *)
  match t with
  | tm_bvar _ => []
  | tm_fvar x => [x]
  | tm_abs T t1 => fv_tm t1
  | tm_app t1 t2 => fv_tm t1 ++ fv_tm t2
  | tm_tabs t1 => fv_tm t1
  | tm_tapp t1 T => fv_tm t1
  | tm_choice t1 t2 => fv_tm t1 ++ fv_tm t2
  end.
```

But the lemma is also used with U = FVar X (in the T_TAbs opening), which is also bvar-free. So we can restrict the semantic substitution lemma to hypotheses where U has no free bvars! Do we have a predicate for "no free bvars"? That's `lc_ty_at 0`? No, lc_ty_at allows bvars < K; at K=0 it forbids all free bvars but allows FVars. Yes! `lc_ty_at 0 U` means U has no free bvars (FVars allowed, under binders bvars are bound). So lc_ty_at 0 is exactly "bvar-free". And in T_TApp, wf_ty Δ U0 implies lc_ty_at 0 U0 (the wf_ty_lc lemma!). 

So the semantic substitution lemma can be stated for U with lc_ty_at 0 U (bvar-free), and then `V U δ_cur ρ = V U δ_out ρ` should hold because U has no free bvars — provable by induction on U (the All case: U = Ty_All T; V's semantics uses the body T with extended δ and quantifies over all R; the body's free bvars are < 1, and the environment extension makes the front entry arbitrary; hmm, we need "V T (extend_b R δ1) ρ = V T (extend_b R δ2) ρ" when T is lc_ty_at 1 — i.e., V depends only on the first K entries of δ where K is the bvar bound of T. Let's prove a general "V depends only on δ restricted to the lc bound": if lc_ty_at K T and δ1, δ2 agree on indices < K, then V T δ1 ρ = V T δ2 ρ. Proof by induction on T... For T = Ty_All T', lc_ty_at K (Ty_All T') → lc_ty_at (S K) T'; V unfolds with extend_b R δ1 vs extend_b R δ2; these agree on indices < S K if δ1,δ2 agree on < K ✓; IH. For Ty_BVar i with i<K, δ1 i = δ2 i ✓. For arrow, IH. So the lemma holds. Then lc_ty_at 0 U means it depends on no δ entries, so V U is δ-independent. 

So the semantic substitution lemma:

`Lemma V_open_ty_rec_subst : forall T k U δ ρ v1 v2, lc_ty_at 0 U -> V (open_ty_rec k U T) δ ρ v1 v2 ↔ V T (replace_at k (V U δ ρ) δ) ρ v1 v2.`

Wait, but we also need to handle the case where U is a FVar X and we want to use the *ρ*-based interpretation. The earlier opening lemma with FVar X: `V (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) δ ρ ↔ V T (replace_at k (ρ X) δ) ρ`. And note `V (FVar X) δ ρ = ρ X`. So the general substitution lemma with U := FVar X gives `replace_at k (V (FVar X) δ ρ) δ = replace_at k (ρ X) δ` — exactly the opening lemma! So the general lemma subsumes it, provided lc_ty_at 0 (FVar X) holds ✓. And in the All case of the general lemma, the condition `V U (extend_b R δ) ρ = V U δ ρ` is discharged by δ-independence of U (lc_ty_at 0 U). Wait, but in the All case of the induction, the environment is extend_b R δ, and U's lc is at 0, so it's independent of any δ. ✓.

Hmm, but hold on: the semantic substitution lemma's RHS uses `V U δ ρ` where δ is the *original* environment. In the All case, the recursive IH is applied at (k+1) with environment `extend_b R δ`, and would produce `replace_at (k+1) (V U (extend_b R δ) ρ) (extend_b R δ)`; δ-independence gives V U (extend_b R δ) ρ = V U δ ρ ✓; and the environment equality as computed. 

So we have a single powerful lemma:

```
Lemma V_open_ty_rec : forall T k U δ ρ v1 v2,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  V (open_ty_rec k U T) δ ρ v1 v2 <->
  V T (fun i => if Nat.eqb i k then V U δ ρ else δ i) ρ v1 v2.
```

And its corollary for FVar X:
`V (open_ty_rec k (FVar X) T) δ ρ ↔ V T (replace_at k (ρ X) δ) ρ`, since V (FVar X) δ ρ = ρ X.

Wait, but in the general lemma, `lc_ty_at 0 U` — in the T_TAbs case, U is FVar X ✓. In the T_TApp case, U is the source type argument with wf_ty Δ U0 → lc_ty_at 0 U0 ✓. But careful: U0 in the T_TApp case might be under term binders but not type binders; wf_ty Δ U0 holds at the judgment. Fine.

Hmm, but why did we need `lc_ty_at 0 U` in the base case? Recall base case Ty_BVar i: LHS = if i=k then V U δ ρ else δ i; RHS = if i=k then V U δ ρ else δ i — no condition needed! The condition was only for the All case where the environment under the binder changes. With lc_ty_at 0 U, V U is δ-independent. Good.

So the key semantic lemmas:

Similarly in the T_Abs case: x fresh beyond L and the free term vars of t2 (for the subst commutation), and maybe free type vars? The term subst commutation needs x not free in t2 (term fvars); the env condition for other variables y: lookup y (x::Γ) = lookup y Γ if y ≠ x; for y=x we handle. But also, the environment condition for y could be affected if x appears in Γ (then lookup x Γ = Some T_old but lookup x (x::Γ) = Some T1). We handle y=x by the new condition. But the IH's conclusion at the body uses γ' with γ' x = a; the environment condition needs to hold for all y in the context (x::Γ). For y≠x, lookup y (x::Γ) = lookup y Γ (since y≠x; Nat.eqb y x = false) and γ' y = γ y. ✓. No further freshness needed. Also, the commutation `tm_subst_env γ' (tm_ty_subst_env σ t2')` needs γ' to agree with γ on the free term vars of the type-substituted t2; type substitution doesn't change term fvars, so need x ∉ fv_tm t2. Choose x fresh for L ++ fv_tm t2. ✓.

Also, we need `tm_subst_env γ' (open_tm ... (fvar x)) = open_tm (tm_subst_env γ (...) ) a` — this uses the lemma `tm_subst_env γ' (open_tm_rec 0 (fvar x) T) = open_tm_rec 0 (γ' x) (tm_subst_env γ' T)` requiring all γ' values lc. γ' x = a is a value→lc, and γ values lc ✓.

Now let's also handle the C relation's "value reduction" requirement: C T δ ρ t1 t2 := ∃ v1 v2, t1 -->* v1 ∧ t2 -->* v2 ∧ V T δ ρ v1 v2. In the T_App case, IH1 gives the reductions. We combine with multi-step transitivity and congruence lemmas.

Let me now also think about whether `multi` has transitivity: 
```
Lemma multi_trans : forall x y z, x -->* y -> y -->* z -> x -->* z.
```
Induction on the first derivation. ✓.

Congruence lemmas needed:
- `multi_app_l : t1 -->* t1' -> locally_closed_tm t2 -> tm_app t1 t2 -->* tm_app t1' t2`.
- `multi_app_r : value v1 -> t2 -->* t2' -> tm_app v1 t2 -->* tm_app v1 t2'`.
- `multi_tapp : t -->* t' -> locally_closed_ty U -> tm_tapp t U -->* tm_tapp t' U`.
- maybe `multi_choice_l/r` not needed.
- `step_multi : t --> t' -> t -->* t'`.
We'll need `multi_refl`, `multi_step` constructors.

For `multi_tapp`: ST_TApp with lc_ty U. ✓.

Now, the `V` value lemma:

Lemma V_value : forall T δ ρ v1 v2,
  (forall i x1 x2, δ i x1 x2 -> value x1 /\ value x2) ->
  (forall X x1 x2, ρ X x1 x2 -> value x1 /\ value x2) ->
  V T δ ρ v1 v2 -> value v1 /\ value v2.
```
Proof by induction on T. Cases:
- BVar/FVar: apply hypotheses.
- Arrow: V gives value v1 ∧ value v2 directly.
- All: V gives value v1 ∧ value v2 directly. 
Wait, actually in my V definition I wrote `value v1 /\ value v2 /\ ...` for arrow and all, so the lemma is trivial for those. Hmm, then do we even need the hypotheses? Only for BVar/FVar. Yes.

Hmm, but the arrow case's V definition requires `value v1` AND shape `exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1`. Since value v1 could also be tm_tabs, we include the shape. Similarly All requires shape tm_tabs. Fine.

### The V definition and Coq acceptance

Let me write V carefully. The Fixpoint must be structurally recursive on T. The arrow case: recursive calls V T1 and V T2 (subterms ✓). The All case: V T' (subterm ✓). The C relation is a Definition using V. The V definition contains `exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ ... V T2 ...` — fine.

But there's a subtlety: V T1 appears in the argument position of a forall in the arrow case, and V T2 inside an existential under it. Strict positivity isn't required for Fixpoints (only for inductives), and structural recursion is on T; all recursive calls are on T1/T2/T'. Coq should accept. However, `V` returns Prop and the recursive calls appear under `forall a1 a2, V T1 ... -> ...`; that's fine for a Fixpoint.

Let me write the definitions:

```
Fixpoint V (T : ty) (δ : nat -> rel) (ρ : atom -> rel) (v1 v2 : tm) {struct T} : Prop :=
  match T with
  | Ty_BVar i => δ i v1 v2
  | Ty_FVar X => ρ X v1 v2
  | Ty_Arrow T1 T2 =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (exists T1' b1, v1 = tm_abs T1' b1) /\
      (exists T2' b2, v2 = tm_abs T2' b2) /\
      (forall a1 a2, V T1 δ ρ a1 a2 ->
        exists w1 w2, tm_app v1 a1 -->* w1 /\ tm_app v2 a2 -->* w2 /\ V T2 δ ρ w1 w2)
  | Ty_All T' =>
      value v1 /\ value v2 /\
      (exists s1 s2, v1 = tm_tabs s1 /\ v2 = tm_tabs s2 /\
        forall U1 U2, lc_ty_at 0 U1 -> lc_ty_at 0 U2 ->
        forall R, (forall x1 x2, R x1 x2 -> value x1 /\ value x2) ->
          exists w1 w2,
            open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\
            open_tm_ty s2 U2 -->* w2 /\
            V T' (extend_b R δ) ρ w1 w2)
  end.
```

Coq's guard checker: recursive calls on T1, T2, T'. In the arrow case, the recursive call V T1 is in the domain of an arrow within the constructor arg — the function's decreasing argument is T; each recursive call uses a direct subterm. Should be fine.

Potential issue: `extend_b R δ` — define `Definition extend_b (R : rel) (δ : nat -> rel) : nat -> rel := fun i => match i with 0 => R | S j => δ j end.`

Now, `replace_at k X δ := fun i => if Nat.eqb i k then X else δ i`.

The V_delta_indep lemma:
```
Lemma V_delta_indep : forall T δ1 δ2 ρ v1 v2,
  (forall i, i < ty_bound? ...) 
```
We need the "bvar bound" of T: define `ty_bvar_bound`? For lc_ty_at K T, the relevant bound is K. Let's state:
`lc_ty_at K T -> (forall i, i < K -> δ1 i = δ2 i) -> (V T δ1 ρ v1 v2 <-> V T δ2 ρ v1 v2)`.
But for the All case, lc_ty_at K (Ty_All T') gives lc_ty_at (S K) T'; the V body uses extend_b R δ1 and extend_b R δ2; these agree on indices < S K if δ1,δ2 agree on < K: index 0 both R; index S j: δ1 j = δ2 j if j < K ✓. Good. So the lemma holds by induction on T. Note the V definition's All case quantifies over R and value-relatedness; identical. ✓.

Then `lc_ty_at 0 U -> V U δ1 ρ = V U δ2 ρ` via V_delta_indep with K=0. (Need extensionality of the iff → equality of Props; use `propext`? In Coq without axioms, we can avoid needing equality by stating the lemma in terms of iff and rewriting with the iff where needed. But `replace_at` involves `V U δ ρ` as a *relation* (a function value), so we need equality of the relations `V U δ1 ρ` and `V U δ2 ρ` as functions to conclude env equality. Hmm. In the V_open_ty_rec lemma, we need to compare `replace_at k (V U (extend_b R δ) ρ) (extend_b R δ)` with `extend_b R (replace_at k (V U δ ρ) δ)` pointwise, i.e., at index k we need the relation equality `V U (extend_b R δ) ρ = V U δ ρ`. As functions, this requires functional extensionality (over tm × tm) plus propext (iff→eq). Ugh, axioms? `functional_extensionality` is not provable in Coq without an axiom; but `Prop` extensionality (propext) is an axiom too. Hmm, but the task forbids "added axioms" — using the standard library's `FunctionalExtensionality` or `PropositionalExtensionality` would be adding axioms (though from the stdlib). That's likely not allowed ("must not use ... added axioms").

Let's avoid relation equality. We can reformulate the semantic substitution lemma differently: instead of `replace_at k (V U δ ρ) δ`, use a *pointwise* environment and state the equivalence. Or define the semantics' All-case environment update via a function that takes the relation as a function argument; the comparison at index k is between two relations, but we only ever *apply* the environment at an index to a pair (v1,v2). So we can state:

`V (open_ty_rec k U T) δ ρ v1 v2 ↔ V T (fun i a b => if i =? k then V U δ ρ a b else δ i a b) ρ v1 v2`.

Here the environment is written directly as a lambda over i, a, b, avoiding equality of relations: at index k it applies V U δ ρ to a,b. In the All case of the induction, we need to show the environments match pointwise, which reduces to arithmetic and beta; no propext needed if we're careful with how the environments are written. Let's see:

Define `extend_b R δ := fun i a b => match i with 0 => R a b | S j => δ j a b end`. Then the All case's LHS environment is `extend_b R_l δ`, and the RHS environment at the body is `extend_b R_l (fun i a b => if i=?k then V U δ ρ a b else δ i a b)`. The IH at k+1 and environment (extend_b R_l δ) gives RHS `fun i a b => if i =? (k+1) then V U (extend_b R_l δ) ρ a b else extend_b R_l δ i a b`. We need this to be equivalent (pointwise) to `extend_b R_l (fun i a b => if i=?k then V U δ ρ a b else δ i a b)`. At i=0: LHS: 0 ≠ k+1 (unless k+1=0 impossible) → extend_b R_l δ 0 a b = R_l a b; RHS = R_l a b ✓. At i=S j: LHS: if S j =? k+1 then V U (extend_b R_l δ) ρ a b else extend_b R_l δ (S j) a b = if j=?k then V U (extend_b R_l δ) ρ a b else δ j a b; RHS: extend_b R_l (...) (S j) a b = (if j=?k then V U δ ρ a b else δ j a b). So at j=k, we need `V U (extend_b R_l δ) ρ a b ↔ V U δ ρ a b`, which we have by V_delta_indep since lc_ty_at 0 U. Using the iff, we can rewrite the goal (the environments are functions; but we're proving `V T env1 ρ v1 v2 ↔ V T env2 ρ v1 v2` where env1/env2 differ pointwise only by an iff at one index). Hmm, to prove this we need a "V depends on δ pointwise-up-to-iff" lemma: if for all i a b, δ1 i a b ↔ δ2 i a b, then V T δ1 ρ v1 v2 ↔ V T δ2 ρ v1 v2. This is provable by induction on T without needing relation equality — just use the pointwise iff and IH. The arrow/all cases: same ρ, and the δ enters only through V at subterms and the extend_b; the pointwise iff lifts through extend_b. ✓. So define:

```
Lemma V_delta_iff : forall T δ1 δ2 ρ v1 v2,
  (forall i x1 x2, δ1 i x1 x2 <-> δ2 i x1 x2) ->
  V T δ1 ρ v1 v2 <-> V T δ2 ρ v1 v2.
```
and
```
Lemma V_rho_iff : forall T δ ρ1 ρ2 v1 v2,
  (forall X x1 x2, ρ1 X x1 x2 <-> ρ2 X x1 x2) ->
  V T δ ρ1 v1 v2 <-> V T δ ρ2 v1 v2.
```

Then in the V_open_ty_rec proof, at the j=k point we need to show the environments are pointwise iff; this reduces to `V U (extend_b R_l δ) ρ a b ↔ V U δ ρ a b` which follows from V_delta_iff applied to U with the pointwise agreement of extend_b R_l δ and δ *only on indices < 0*?? No: extend_b R_l δ and δ differ at index 0 generally! Wait, U has lc_ty_at 0, so by V_delta_indep (with K=0), V U is independent of δ entirely; we can use V_delta_indep to get both sides equal to V U (fun _ _ _ => False) ρ. But V_delta_indep was stated with equality of δ at indices; let me restate it via iff:

```
Lemma V_delta_indep : forall T K δ1 δ2 ρ v1 v2,
  lc_ty_at K T ->
  (forall i, i < K -> forall x1 x2, δ1 i x1 x2 <-> δ2 i x1 x2) ->
  V T δ1 ρ v1 v2 <-> V T δ2 ρ v1 v2.
```
Proof induction on T; All case: lc gives S K body; extend_b R δ1 vs extend_b R δ2 agree pointwise for i < S K: i=0 both R; i=S j with j<K: δ1 j ↔ δ2 j ✓. IH. BVar: i<K gives the iff. Others: IH. ✓ no equality needed.

Then for lc_ty_at 0 U: any two δ agree trivially on no indices, so `V U δ1 ρ a b ↔ V U δ2 ρ a b` for all δ1,δ2. 

So the semantic substitution lemma can be stated with the environment lambda:

```
Lemma V_open_ty_rec : forall T k U δ ρ v1 v2,
  lc_ty_at 0 U ->
  (V (open_ty_rec k U T) δ ρ v1 v2 <->
   V T (fun i a b => if Nat.eqb i k then V U δ ρ a b else δ i a b) ρ v1 v2).
```

Proof by induction on T:
- BVar i: simpl. LHS: δ i v1 v2 if i≠k, or V U δ ρ v1 v2 if i=k. RHS: (fun i a b => if i=?k then V U δ ρ a b else δ i a b) i v1 v2 = same. Need the if computation; destruct (Nat.eqb i k) and simpl. Should be reflexivity essentially.
- FVar: both ρ X.
- Arrow: LHS V (arrow (open_rec k U T1) (open_rec k U T2)) δ ρ = value /\ value /\ shape /\ forall a1 a2, V (open_rec k U T1) δ ρ a1 a2 -> ∃w, ... V (open_rec k U T2) δ ρ w1 w2. RHS with env' at T1,T2: by IH (both directions), the argument relation matches and the result relation matches. So the arrows are logically equivalent. The `value`, shape components identical. Need to build the iff manually: split; intros; destruct; etc. The forall part: IH1 and IH2 give iff; we can use `split; intros H a1 a2 Ha; [rewrite <- IH1 in Ha | ...]`. Manageable.
- All: LHS V (Ty_All (open_ty_rec (k+1) U T')) δ ρ = value/\value/\exists s1 s2, ... forall U1 U2 lc R VR, ∃w1 w2, open_tm_ty s1 U1 -->* w1 /\ ... /\ V (open_ty_rec (k+1) U T') (extend_b R δ) ρ w1 w2.
  RHS V (Ty_All T') env' ρ = ... /\ V T' (extend_b R env') ρ w1 w2.
  By IH with k+1, environment (extend_b R δ): `V (open_ty_rec (k+1) U T') (extend_b R δ) ρ w1 w2 ↔ V T' (env'' ) ρ w1 w2` where env'' i a b = if i =? (k+1) then V U (extend_b R δ) ρ a b else (extend_b R δ) i a b.
  Need env'' pointwise iff `extend_b R env'`:
  - i=0: env'' 0 = (0 =? k+1 ? ... : extend_b R δ 0) = R (since k+1≠0); extend_b R env' 0 = R. ✓.
  - i=S j: env'' (S j) a b = if S j =? k+1 then V U (extend_b R δ) ρ a b else (extend_b R δ) (S j) a b = if j=?k then V U (extend_b R δ) ρ a b else δ j a b. extend_b R env' (S j) a b = env' j a b = if j=?k then V U δ ρ a b else δ j a b. At j=k, need V U (extend_b R δ) ρ a b ↔ V U δ ρ a b, which follows from lc_ty_at 0 U by V_delta_indep with K=0 (both related to any environment, e.g., themselves). So pointwise iff. Then apply IH and V_delta_iff... wait, we have the iff between V T' env'' and V T' (extend_b R env') via V_delta_iff (pointwise iff hypothesis). So combine: LHS body ↔ env'' ↔ extend_b R env'. ✓.
  This is doable but somewhat involved in Coq. We'll write helper lemmas for the environment pointwise equivalences, or just prove directly.

Maybe a cleaner approach: instead of proving V_open_ty_rec by induction with these environment manipulations, we can define V with environments as *functions into rel* and use extensionality-free rewrites via the V_delta_iff/V_rho_iff lemmas. I think it's manageable with careful Ltac.

Alternatively, define `rel` as a record with a carrier Prop and use setoid? No, extensionality again.

Hmm, another approach: define the environment `replace_at` and use *pointwise* statements everywhere; define V's All case to take the environment as a function `nat -> tm -> tm -> Prop` and always compare pointwise. That's what we're doing.

Now, I realize there's another way to avoid the semantic substitution lemma complexities entirely: define the semantics of types *with an explicit type substitution environment* — i.e., interpret types relative to a "semantic environment" that maps each *type expression* (or each fvar) to a relation, and define `V (open_ty_rec k U T)` vs `V T` by structurally matching. The lemma is essentially unavoidable. Let's just do it.

Let me now also think about the `V_fv_agree` lemma (ρ congruence). Similar issues: we need ρ pointwise iff on the free fvars of T. State:

```
Lemma V_rho_indep : forall T δ ρ1 ρ2 v1 v2,
  (forall X, In X (fv_ty T) -> forall x1 x2, ρ1 X x1 x2 <-> ρ2 X x1 x2) ->
  V T δ ρ1 v1 v2 <-> V T δ ρ2 v1 v2.
```
Proof by induction on T. FVar X: apply H with In X [X] ✓. All: fv_ty (Ty_All T') = fv_ty T'; IH. Arrow: IH both. ✓.

Then the special case: X ∉ fv_ty T and ρ' = fun Y => if Y =? X then R else ρ Y (pointwise on relations: `fun a b => if Y =? X then R a b else ρ Y a b`). For Y ∈ fv_ty T, Y ≠ X, so ρ' Y a b = ρ Y a b (needs Nat.eqb Y X = false from Y≠X; get from `Nat.eqb_eq`/`Nat.eqb_neq`). We'll prove `ρ_upd_agree` helper.

Now, the fundamental theorem's environment condition uses C at types Tx from Γ. When we change ρ to ρ', we need C congruence, which follows from V_rho_indep by unfolding C.

Also, the value-relatedness of ρ' for the IH: need `∀X a b, ρ' X a b -> value a ∧ value b`. Given ρ's and R's. Since ρ' X = R if X =? X_target... For each X, either X = X_target (then R) or not (then ρ X). ✓.

Now, let's also think about `wf_ty_lc`:

Lemma wf_ty_lc : forall Δ T, wf_ty Δ T -> lc_ty_at 0 T.
